Maybe not in those exact words, but that’s the feeling. Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that, at least in my more sensible moments. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand out on the table, I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not my face, not what I’m saying, just that scar. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. Like they see my wrist before they see me. And then whether they’re wondering how I got it or just thinking it looks unattractive… I don’t know, I start filling in the blanks myself. But the main thing is that I feel like they’re noticing it and judging me because it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging your overall appearance because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think in a way I do. Not that I imagine they’re judging every single part of me from head to toe, exactly, but it feels like the scar changes how they see me overall. Like instead of just seeing me as I am, they notice that first, and then everything about me feels sort of… affected by it.\n\nI know, if I step back, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that here, and I do try to remind myself of it. But when I’m at church, or when I’m sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, and I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, it’s hard not to think it signifies something, even if I couldn’t say exactly what — just that I look marked somehow, or less presentable. But the strongest part is really that I feel like they notice it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar on your wrist when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I can stop for a little while if I’m busy or if I really force myself to think about something else, but it comes back very easily. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. When I look down at my wrist, it just catches my eye, and to me it looks so obvious, even though part of me knows it probably isn’t as bad as it seems in my own mind.\n\nBut I think the concern gets much worse when I imagine other people seeing it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before anything else. It’s hard for me to shake that feeling once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your wrist if you could somehow make the scar less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I mean, that’s what I keep telling myself—if it were just a little paler, or smoother, or somehow didn’t catch my eye so much, then maybe I could stop thinking about it all the time.\n\nBecause I do know, rationally, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But then when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is out on the table, it feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that, at least in my more sensible moments. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand out on the table, I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. I can say that here, and I do try to remind myself of it. But when I’m at church, or when I’m sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.     "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s… a little of both, I think, but if I had to say what bothers me most, it’s that it feels like it draws attention to me. I do think it looks unattractive when I look at it — or at least, it looks that way to me. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks in my own mind, and I can sort of tell myself that when I’m being rational.

But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t hold onto that thought. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says anything, I’ll think, “They saw it,” or “They’re wondering what happened,” or “They’re looking at my wrist instead of listening to me.” So it’s not just that I think it’s ugly, exactly. It’s more that I feel exposed by it, like it announces itself before I do.

========== INPUT [doctor] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it feels to me. I can say that out loud and part of me knows it. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they see now when they look at me.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing to even say, because I’m 64 years old and I feel like I ought to be able to just ignore something like that. But I’ll be sitting there holding my cards, or reaching for a hymnal, and suddenly I’m thinking, “They’re looking at my wrist.” It’s just been bothering me more than I expected, and I guess I wanted to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar been affecting your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little silly admitting how much. I’m still doing most things, but it’s started interfering more than I’d like.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, for instance, I’ve been late turning things in a few times because I get so caught up before I go in or before I sit down with paperwork, fussing with sleeves or bracelets or trying to make sure my wrist won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m not as focused as I should be. I’ll tell myself, “Just get the figures done,” but then I look down and there it is, and I start adjusting things again.\n\nAnd socially… yes. I’ve skipped choir a couple of times, and I’ve backed out of some theater volunteer commitments too. I enjoy those things, normally, but I start imagining being under the lights or sitting with the choir where people can see my hands, and I just decide it’s easier not to go. Then afterward I feel bad, because I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable or that I don’t care. It’s not that I don’t care—I just get so wrapped up in whether someone will notice the scar that it feels easier to avoid the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar on your wrist mainly about how it looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main part of it. It’s about how it looks, or how I imagine it looks, to other people. I know in my head that the scar on my wrist is probably not as terrible or obvious as it feels to me, and maybe some people don’t even notice it. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and instead I’m thinking about whether my sleeve has ridden up or whether my hand is turned the wrong way. It’s not pain or anything like that. It’s the thought that people can see it and are judging it, or thinking it looks awful, even if no one has actually said anything.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about how they look to others?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly the wrist scar. I mean, I don’t love everything about getting older, of course, but this is the thing I keep getting stuck on.\n\nI know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—at least, I can tell myself that when I’m being reasonable. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my cards in my hand, and I just feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before anything else. It’s hard for me to shake that feeling once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your wrist if you could somehow make the scar less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I mean, that’s what I keep telling myself—if it were just a little paler, or smoother, or somehow didn’t catch my eye so much, then maybe I could stop thinking about it all the time.\n\nBecause I do know, rationally, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But then when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is out on the table, it feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like before they see me, they see that. And I know that may not be true, but it feels true in the moment.\n\nSo yes… if it were less noticeable, I imagine I’d feel less exposed. Maybe I wouldn’t be so aware of my sleeve or where my wrist is or whether someone is looking. I think I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that people will think there's something wrong with you or that you're unattractive because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry that people will think there’s something wrong with me because of it. Maybe not in those exact words, but that’s the feeling. Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that, at least in my more sensible moments. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand out on the table, I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not my face, not what I’m saying, just that scar. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. Like they see my wrist before they see me. And then whether they’re wondering how I got it or just thinking it looks unattractive… I don’t know, I start filling in the blanks myself. But the main thing is that I feel like they’re noticing it and judging me because it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging your overall appearance because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think in a way I do. Not that I imagine they’re judging every single part of me from head to toe, exactly, but it feels like the scar changes how they see me overall. Like instead of just seeing me as I am, they notice that first, and then everything about me feels sort of… affected by it.\n\nI know, if I step back, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that here, and I do try to remind myself of it. But when I’m at church, or when I’m sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, and I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, it’s hard not to think it signifies something, even if I couldn’t say exactly what — just that I look marked somehow, or less presentable. But the strongest part is really that I feel like they notice it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar on your wrist when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I can stop for a little while if I’m busy or if I really force myself to think about something else, but it comes back very easily. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t want to be thinking about it as much as I do, but once I’m aware of it, it’s hard to put it out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the wrist scar affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. I still try to do the things I normally enjoy, but the scar has started getting in the way of them. With choir, for example, I’ve skipped a couple of times because I couldn’t stop thinking about whether people would notice my wrist when I was holding music or standing near others. And with the theater volunteer work, I’ve backed out of commitments because I got so caught up trying to figure out what I’d wear, or how I’d keep my wrist turned or covered, that it just felt easier not to go.\n\nIt’s affected work too, even though that’s not exactly pleasurable. I do bookkeeping, and I’ve been late turning some things in because I lose time fussing over whether my wrist will show if I have to drop something off or be around people. I know that sounds like a small thing, but it can take up so much of my attention. I’ll mean to just check how it looks for a minute, and then suddenly I’ve spent too long on it and I’m behind.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected my participation. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. When I look down at my wrist, it just catches my eye, and to me it looks so obvious, even though part of me knows it probably isn’t as bad as it seems in my own mind.\n\nBut I think the concern gets much worse when I imagine other people seeing it. If I somehow knew, absolutely knew, that nobody else noticed it at all, I might be less upset. But that’s the hard part — when I’m at church or playing bridge, I can’t seem to believe that. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo I suppose the honest answer is that yes, I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me, but a lot of the distress comes from thinking other people must be seeing it the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your wrist scar if you were alone, without anyone else around to see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would still feel self-conscious about it even if I were alone, though it’s different when no one else is there. When I’m by myself, I still see it. I’ll notice my wrist when I’m washing my hands or just sitting with my arm out, and it kind of pulls my attention to it. I know, in my head, that the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but it doesn’t always feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nBut it gets much stronger when I’m around other people. At church or when I’m playing bridge, that’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. If I were completely alone, I wouldn’t have that part of it, the worrying about what other people are seeing, but I’d still be aware of it and bothered by how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about the scar that it looks unattractive or that it draws attention to you?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little of both, I think, but if I had to say what bothers me most, it’s that it feels like it draws attention to me. I do think it looks unattractive when I look at it — or at least, it looks that way to me. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks in my own mind, and I can sort of tell myself that when I’m being rational.\n\nBut when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t hold onto that thought. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says anything, I’ll think, “They saw it,” or “They’re wondering what happened,” or “They’re looking at my wrist instead of listening to me.” So it’s not just that I think it’s ugly, exactly. It’s more that I feel exposed by it, like it announces itself before I do.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it probably would decrease some, if I truly knew I wasn’t being judged or evaluated. I don’t think it would disappear completely, because I still notice the scar myself. I can look at my wrist and think it looks more obvious than it probably is, even when I’m trying to be reasonable about it.

But the worst part is when I’m with other people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge. That’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says a word, I find myself assuming they’ve seen it and are thinking something about it.

So if I were in a situation where I somehow knew people weren’t judging me, I think I’d feel less tense and less exposed. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out.

I mean, that’s what I keep telling myself—if it were just a little paler, or smoother, or somehow didn’t catch my eye so much, then maybe I could stop thinking about it all the time.\n\nBecause I do know, rationally, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t want to be thinking about it as much as I do, but once I’m aware of it, it’s hard to put it out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the wrist scar affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. I still try to do the things I normally enjoy, but the scar has started getting in the way of them. I know that sounds like a small thing, but it can take up so much of my attention. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think I would. Maybe not in the exact same way as if they were already looking at me, but if I knew I was about to meet someone I really admire or respect, I’d start thinking about it ahead of time. Like, even sitting privately, I’d probably glance down at my wrist or imagine what it’s going to look like when I shake hands or sit across from them.

I know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me — I can say that when I’m calm — but it still feels very noticeable to me. And when I’m around people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like their eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So if it were someone I respected, I think that would make me even more aware of it, because I’d want to make a good impression and not have them focusing on my wrist or wondering about it.

So yes, even if no one could see it right that second, just knowing I was about to be seen by someone whose opinion mattered to me would probably make me anxious about it.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.22, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it feels to me. I can say that out loud and part of me knows it. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they see now when they look at me.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing to even say, because I’m 64 years old and I feel like I ought to be able to just ignore something like that. But I’ll be sitting there holding my cards, or reaching for a hymnal, and suddenly I’m thinking, “They’re looking at my wrist.” It’s just been bothering me more than I expected, and I guess I wanted to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar been affecting your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little silly admitting how much. I’m still doing most things, but it’s started interfering more than I’d like.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, for instance, I’ve been late turning things in a few times because I get so caught up before I go in or before I sit down with paperwork, fussing with sleeves or bracelets or trying to make sure my wrist won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m not as focused as I should be. I’ll tell myself, “Just get the figures done,” but then I look down and there it is, and I start adjusting things again.\n\nAnd socially… yes. I’ve skipped choir a couple of times, and I’ve backed out of some theater volunteer commitments too. I enjoy those things, normally, but I start imagining being under the lights or sitting with the choir where people can see my hands, and I just decide it’s easier not to go. Then afterward I feel bad, because I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable or that I don’t care. It’s not that I don’t care—I just get so wrapped up in whether someone will notice the scar that it feels easier to avoid the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar on your wrist mainly about how it looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main part of it. It’s about how it looks, or how I imagine it looks, to other people. I know in my head that the scar on my wrist is probably not as terrible or obvious as it feels to me, and maybe some people don’t even notice it. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and instead I’m thinking about whether my sleeve has ridden up or whether my hand is turned the wrong way. It’s not pain or anything like that. It’s the thought that people can see it and are judging it, or thinking it looks awful, even if no one has actually said anything.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about how they look to others?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly the wrist scar. I mean, I don’t love everything about getting older, of course, but this is the thing I keep getting stuck on.\n\nI know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—at least, I can tell myself that when I’m being reasonable. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my cards in my hand, and I just feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before anything else. It’s hard for me to shake that feeling once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your wrist if you could somehow make the scar less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I mean, that’s what I keep telling myself—if it were just a little paler, or smoother, or somehow didn’t catch my eye so much, then maybe I could stop thinking about it all the time.\n\nBecause I do know, rationally, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But then when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is out on the table, it feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like before they see me, they see that. And I know that may not be true, but it feels true in the moment.\n\nSo yes… if it were less noticeable, I imagine I’d feel less exposed. Maybe I wouldn’t be so aware of my sleeve or where my wrist is or whether someone is looking. I think I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that people will think there's something wrong with you or that you're unattractive because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry that people will think there’s something wrong with me because of it. Maybe not in those exact words, but that’s the feeling. Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that, at least in my more sensible moments. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand out on the table, I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not my face, not what I’m saying, just that scar. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. Like they see my wrist before they see me. And then whether they’re wondering how I got it or just thinking it looks unattractive… I don’t know, I start filling in the blanks myself. But the main thing is that I feel like they’re noticing it and judging me because it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging your overall appearance because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think in a way I do. Not that I imagine they’re judging every single part of me from head to toe, exactly, but it feels like the scar changes how they see me overall. Like instead of just seeing me as I am, they notice that first, and then everything about me feels sort of… affected by it.\n\nI know, if I step back, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that here, and I do try to remind myself of it. But when I’m at church, or when I’m sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, and I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, it’s hard not to think it signifies something, even if I couldn’t say exactly what — just that I look marked somehow, or less presentable. But the strongest part is really that I feel like they notice it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar on your wrist when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I can stop for a little while if I’m busy or if I really force myself to think about something else, but it comes back very easily. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t want to be thinking about it as much as I do, but once I’m aware of it, it’s hard to put it out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the wrist scar affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. I still try to do the things I normally enjoy, but the scar has started getting in the way of them. With choir, for example, I’ve skipped a couple of times because I couldn’t stop thinking about whether people would notice my wrist when I was holding music or standing near others. And with the theater volunteer work, I’ve backed out of commitments because I got so caught up trying to figure out what I’d wear, or how I’d keep my wrist turned or covered, that it just felt easier not to go.\n\nIt’s affected work too, even though that’s not exactly pleasurable. I do bookkeeping, and I’ve been late turning some things in because I lose time fussing over whether my wrist will show if I have to drop something off or be around people. I know that sounds like a small thing, but it can take up so much of my attention. I’ll mean to just check how it looks for a minute, and then suddenly I’ve spent too long on it and I’m behind.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected my participation. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. When I look down at my wrist, it just catches my eye, and to me it looks so obvious, even though part of me knows it probably isn’t as bad as it seems in my own mind.\n\nBut I think the concern gets much worse when I imagine other people seeing it. If I somehow knew, absolutely knew, that nobody else noticed it at all, I might be less upset. But that’s the hard part — when I’m at church or playing bridge, I can’t seem to believe that. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo I suppose the honest answer is that yes, I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me, but a lot of the distress comes from thinking other people must be seeing it the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your wrist scar if you were alone, without anyone else around to see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would still feel self-conscious about it even if I were alone, though it’s different when no one else is there. When I’m by myself, I still see it. I’ll notice my wrist when I’m washing my hands or just sitting with my arm out, and it kind of pulls my attention to it. I know, in my head, that the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but it doesn’t always feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nBut it gets much stronger when I’m around other people. At church or when I’m playing bridge, that’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. If I were completely alone, I wouldn’t have that part of it, the worrying about what other people are seeing, but I’d still be aware of it and bothered by how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about the scar that it looks unattractive or that it draws attention to you?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little of both, I think, but if I had to say what bothers me most, it’s that it feels like it draws attention to me. I do think it looks unattractive when I look at it — or at least, it looks that way to me. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks in my own mind, and I can sort of tell myself that when I’m being rational.\n\nBut when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t hold onto that thought. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says anything, I’ll think, “They saw it,” or “They’re wondering what happened,” or “They’re looking at my wrist instead of listening to me.” So it’s not just that I think it’s ugly, exactly. It’s more that I feel exposed by it, like it announces itself before I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the wrist scar would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would decrease some, if I truly knew I wasn’t being judged or evaluated. I don’t think it would disappear completely, because I still notice the scar myself. I can look at my wrist and think it looks more obvious than it probably is, even when I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut the worst part is when I’m with other people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge. That’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says a word, I find myself assuming they’ve seen it and are thinking something about it.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I somehow knew people weren’t judging me, I think I’d feel less tense and less exposed. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your wrist scar if you were in a private setting where no one could see it, but you were about to meet someone you admire or respect?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe not in the exact same way as if they were already looking at me, but if I knew I was about to meet someone I really admire or respect, I’d start thinking about it ahead of time. Like, even sitting privately, I’d probably glance down at my wrist or imagine what it’s going to look like when I shake hands or sit across from them.\n\nI know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me — I can say that when I’m calm — but it still feels very noticeable to me. And when I’m around people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like their eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So if it were someone I respected, I think that would make me even more aware of it, because I’d want to make a good impression and not have them focusing on my wrist or wondering about it.\n\nSo yes, even if no one could see it right that second, just knowing I was about to be seen by someone whose opinion mattered to me would probably make me anxious about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, and I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, it’s hard not to think it signifies something, even if I couldn’t say exactly what — just that I look marked somehow, or less presentable. But the strongest part is really that I feel like they notice it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar on your wrist when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I can stop for a little while if I’m busy or if I really force myself to think about something else, but it comes back very easily. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t want to be thinking about it as much as I do, but once I’m aware of it, it’s hard to put it out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the wrist scar affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. I still try to do the things I normally enjoy, but the scar has started getting in the way of them. With choir, for example, I’ve skipped a couple of times because I couldn’t stop thinking about whether people would notice my wrist when I was holding music or standing near others. And with the theater volunteer work, I’ve backed out of commitments because I got so caught up trying to figure out what I’d wear, or how I’d keep my wrist turned or covered, that it just felt easier not to go.\n\nIt’s affected work too, even though that’s not exactly pleasurable. I do bookkeeping, and I’ve been late turning some things in because I lose time fussing over whether my wrist will show if I have to drop something off or be around people. I know that sounds like a small thing, but it can take up so much of my attention. I’ll mean to just check how it looks for a minute, and then suddenly I’ve spent too long on it and I’m behind.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected my participation. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. When I look down at my wrist, it just catches my eye, and to me it looks so obvious, even though part of me knows it probably isn’t as bad as it seems in my own mind.\n\nBut I think the concern gets much worse when I imagine other people seeing it. If I somehow knew, absolutely knew, that nobody else noticed it at all, I might be less upset. But that’s the hard part — when I’m at church or playing bridge, I can’t seem to believe that. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo I suppose the honest answer is that yes, I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me, but a lot of the distress comes from thinking other people must be seeing it the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your wrist scar if you were alone, without anyone else around to see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would still feel self-conscious about it even if I were alone, though it’s different when no one else is there. When I’m by myself, I still see it. I’ll notice my wrist when I’m washing my hands or just sitting with my arm out, and it kind of pulls my attention to it. I know, in my head, that the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but it doesn’t always feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nBut it gets much stronger when I’m around other people. At church or when I’m playing bridge, that’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. If I were completely alone, I wouldn’t have that part of it, the worrying about what other people are seeing, but I’d still be aware of it and bothered by how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about the scar that it looks unattractive or that it draws attention to you?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little of both, I think, but if I had to say what bothers me most, it’s that it feels like it draws attention to me. I do think it looks unattractive when I look at it — or at least, it looks that way to me. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks in my own mind, and I can sort of tell myself that when I’m being rational.\n\nBut when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t hold onto that thought. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says anything, I’ll think, “They saw it,” or “They’re wondering what happened,” or “They’re looking at my wrist instead of listening to me.” So it’s not just that I think it’s ugly, exactly. It’s more that I feel exposed by it, like it announces itself before I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the wrist scar would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would decrease some, if I truly knew I wasn’t being judged or evaluated. I don’t think it would disappear completely, because I still notice the scar myself. I can look at my wrist and think it looks more obvious than it probably is, even when I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut the worst part is when I’m with other people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge. That’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says a word, I find myself assuming they’ve seen it and are thinking something about it.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I somehow knew people weren’t judging me, I think I’d feel less tense and less exposed. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your wrist scar if you were in a private setting where no one could see it, but you were about to meet someone you admire or respect?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe not in the exact same way as if they were already looking at me, but if I knew I was about to meet someone I really admire or respect, I’d start thinking about it ahead of time. Like, even sitting privately, I’d probably glance down at my wrist or imagine what it’s going to look like when I shake hands or sit across from them.\n\nI know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me — I can say that when I’m calm — but it still feels very noticeable to me. And when I’m around people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like their eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So if it were someone I respected, I think that would make me even more aware of it, because I’d want to make a good impression and not have them focusing on my wrist or wondering about it.\n\nSo yes, even if no one could see it right that second, just knowing I was about to be seen by someone whose opinion mattered to me would probably make me anxious about it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yes… I think it would be reduced quite a bit if I could really be certain of that. I mean, if I somehow knew people weren’t looking at it and thinking something negative, or judging me for it, I probably wouldn’t feel as exposed.

But I don’t know that it would go away completely, because I still see it myself. I know, rationally, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that when I’m alone or when I’m thinking clearly. But then when I’m at church, or playing bridge, it just starts up again — this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.

So if I had real certainty that nobody was evaluating me badly because of it, yes, I’d be less anxious. I think that would take away a lot of the fear. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out more than it should.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I suppose what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time not thinking about this scar on my wrist. I know, logically, it’s probably not nearly as noticeable as it feels to me. I can say that out loud and part of me knows it. But when I’m around people—like at church, or when I’m playing bridge—I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they see now when they look at me.\n\nAnd it’s embarrassing to even say, because I’m 64 years old and I feel like I ought to be able to just ignore something like that. But I’ll be sitting there holding my cards, or reaching for a hymnal, and suddenly I’m thinking, “They’re looking at my wrist.” It’s just been bothering me more than I expected, and I guess I wanted to talk to someone about it.\n\nDoctor: Has this concern about your wrist scar been affecting your daily activities or relationships with others?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, though I feel a little silly admitting how much. I’m still doing most things, but it’s started interfering more than I’d like.\n\nWith my bookkeeping work, for instance, I’ve been late turning things in a few times because I get so caught up before I go in or before I sit down with paperwork, fussing with sleeves or bracelets or trying to make sure my wrist won’t show. And then I lose time, and I’m not as focused as I should be. I’ll tell myself, “Just get the figures done,” but then I look down and there it is, and I start adjusting things again.\n\nAnd socially… yes. I’ve skipped choir a couple of times, and I’ve backed out of some theater volunteer commitments too. I enjoy those things, normally, but I start imagining being under the lights or sitting with the choir where people can see my hands, and I just decide it’s easier not to go. Then afterward I feel bad, because I don’t want people thinking I’m unreliable or that I don’t care. It’s not that I don’t care—I just get so wrapped up in whether someone will notice the scar that it feels easier to avoid the whole situation.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the scar on your wrist mainly about how it looks to others?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s the main part of it. It’s about how it looks, or how I imagine it looks, to other people. I know in my head that the scar on my wrist is probably not as terrible or obvious as it feels to me, and maybe some people don’t even notice it. But when I’m at church or playing bridge, I keep having this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it—like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nI’ll be sitting there trying to pay attention, and instead I’m thinking about whether my sleeve has ridden up or whether my hand is turned the wrong way. It’s not pain or anything like that. It’s the thought that people can see it and are judging it, or thinking it looks awful, even if no one has actually said anything.\n\nDoctor: Is it only your wrist scar that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that you worry about how they look to others?\n\nPatient: It’s really mostly the wrist scar. I mean, I don’t love everything about getting older, of course, but this is the thing I keep getting stuck on.\n\nI know the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me—at least, I can tell myself that when I’m being reasonable. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my cards in my hand, and I just feel like everyone’s eyes go straight to my wrist. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before anything else. It’s hard for me to shake that feeling once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel better about your wrist if you could somehow make the scar less noticeable?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. I mean, that’s what I keep telling myself—if it were just a little paler, or smoother, or somehow didn’t catch my eye so much, then maybe I could stop thinking about it all the time.\n\nBecause I do know, rationally, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But then when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is out on the table, it feels like everyone’s eyes go straight to it. Like before they see me, they see that. And I know that may not be true, but it feels true in the moment.\n\nSo yes… if it were less noticeable, I imagine I’d feel less exposed. Maybe I wouldn’t be so aware of my sleeve or where my wrist is or whether someone is looking. I think I’d feel more like myself again.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry that people will think there's something wrong with you or that you're unattractive because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I do worry that people will think there’s something wrong with me because of it. Maybe not in those exact words, but that’s the feeling. Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I do know that, at least in my more sensible moments. But when I’m at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand out on the table, I get this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Not my face, not what I’m saying, just that scar. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. Like they see my wrist before they see me. And then whether they’re wondering how I got it or just thinking it looks unattractive… I don’t know, I start filling in the blanks myself. But the main thing is that I feel like they’re noticing it and judging me because it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging your overall appearance because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think in a way I do. Not that I imagine they’re judging every single part of me from head to toe, exactly, but it feels like the scar changes how they see me overall. Like instead of just seeing me as I am, they notice that first, and then everything about me feels sort of… affected by it.\n\nI know, if I step back, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that here, and I do try to remind myself of it. But when I’m at church, or when I’m sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. It makes me very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Is your concern about the wrist scar only about how it looks, or is it also about what it might represent or signify to others?\n\nPatient: I think it’s mostly how it looks… or at least that’s where it starts. I don’t think I’m mainly worried that people are making some big story out of it, exactly. It’s more that when I see it, it just seems so obvious to me, so out of place on my wrist, and then I assume it must look that way to everyone else too.\n\nI do know, when I’m being sensible about it, that the scar probably isn’t as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that. But then I’ll be at church, or sitting at bridge with my hand on the table, and I get this awful feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, it’s hard not to think it signifies something, even if I couldn’t say exactly what — just that I look marked somehow, or less presentable. But the strongest part is really that I feel like they notice it immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it difficult to stop thinking about the scar on your wrist when you want to?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I mean, I can stop for a little while if I’m busy or if I really force myself to think about something else, but it comes back very easily. It’s not as if I sit there deciding, “Now I’m going to think about my wrist.” It just sort of intrudes, especially when I’m around people.\n\nLike at church, I’ll be trying to listen or just be present, and then I’ll notice where my hand is, or I’ll wonder if someone near me has seen it, and suddenly that’s all I can think about. Or at bridge, with my hand on the table, it’s even worse because my wrist is right there. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go straight to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And I know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me, but that doesn’t really stop the thought once it gets going.\n\nSo yes, it’s difficult. I don’t want to be thinking about it as much as I do, but once I’m aware of it, it’s hard to put it out of my mind.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about the wrist scar affected your ability to enjoy or participate in activities that you normally find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: Yes… it has, more than I like to admit. I still try to do the things I normally enjoy, but the scar has started getting in the way of them. With choir, for example, I’ve skipped a couple of times because I couldn’t stop thinking about whether people would notice my wrist when I was holding music or standing near others. And with the theater volunteer work, I’ve backed out of commitments because I got so caught up trying to figure out what I’d wear, or how I’d keep my wrist turned or covered, that it just felt easier not to go.\n\nIt’s affected work too, even though that’s not exactly pleasurable. I do bookkeeping, and I’ve been late turning some things in because I lose time fussing over whether my wrist will show if I have to drop something off or be around people. I know that sounds like a small thing, but it can take up so much of my attention. I’ll mean to just check how it looks for a minute, and then suddenly I’ve spent too long on it and I’m behind.\n\nSo yes, I’d say it has affected my participation. I don’t feel like I’ve stopped my whole life, but I’ve definitely avoided things I normally would have done.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still be concerned about your wrist scar if nobody else noticed it?\n\nPatient: I think… I would still notice it myself, yes. I don’t want to pretend I wouldn’t. When I look down at my wrist, it just catches my eye, and to me it looks so obvious, even though part of me knows it probably isn’t as bad as it seems in my own mind.\n\nBut I think the concern gets much worse when I imagine other people seeing it. If I somehow knew, absolutely knew, that nobody else noticed it at all, I might be less upset. But that’s the hard part — when I’m at church or playing bridge, I can’t seem to believe that. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo I suppose the honest answer is that yes, I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me, but a lot of the distress comes from thinking other people must be seeing it the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your wrist scar if you were alone, without anyone else around to see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would still feel self-conscious about it even if I were alone, though it’s different when no one else is there. When I’m by myself, I still see it. I’ll notice my wrist when I’m washing my hands or just sitting with my arm out, and it kind of pulls my attention to it. I know, in my head, that the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but it doesn’t always feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nBut it gets much stronger when I’m around other people. At church or when I’m playing bridge, that’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. If I were completely alone, I wouldn’t have that part of it, the worrying about what other people are seeing, but I’d still be aware of it and bothered by how it looks to me.\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern about the scar that it looks unattractive or that it draws attention to you?\n\nPatient: It’s… a little of both, I think, but if I had to say what bothers me most, it’s that it feels like it draws attention to me. I do think it looks unattractive when I look at it — or at least, it looks that way to me. I know the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks in my own mind, and I can sort of tell myself that when I’m being rational.\n\nBut when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t hold onto that thought. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says anything, I’ll think, “They saw it,” or “They’re wondering what happened,” or “They’re looking at my wrist instead of listening to me.” So it’s not just that I think it’s ugly, exactly. It’s more that I feel exposed by it, like it announces itself before I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your concern about the wrist scar would decrease if you were in a situation where you knew you would not be evaluated or judged by others?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably would decrease some, if I truly knew I wasn’t being judged or evaluated. I don’t think it would disappear completely, because I still notice the scar myself. I can look at my wrist and think it looks more obvious than it probably is, even when I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut the worst part is when I’m with other people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge. That’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says a word, I find myself assuming they’ve seen it and are thinking something about it.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I somehow knew people weren’t judging me, I think I’d feel less tense and less exposed. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your wrist scar if you were in a private setting where no one could see it, but you were about to meet someone you admire or respect?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe not in the exact same way as if they were already looking at me, but if I knew I was about to meet someone I really admire or respect, I’d start thinking about it ahead of time. Like, even sitting privately, I’d probably glance down at my wrist or imagine what it’s going to look like when I shake hands or sit across from them.\n\nI know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me — I can say that when I’m calm — but it still feels very noticeable to me. And when I’m around people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge, I keep feeling like their eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. So if it were someone I respected, I think that would make me even more aware of it, because I’d want to make a good impression and not have them focusing on my wrist or wondering about it.\n\nSo yes, even if no one could see it right that second, just knowing I was about to be seen by someone whose opinion mattered to me would probably make me anxious about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about the wrist scar would be significantly reduced if you could be certain that others do not evaluate you negatively because of it?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it would be reduced quite a bit if I could really be certain of that. I mean, if I somehow knew people weren’t looking at it and thinking something negative, or judging me for it, I probably wouldn’t feel as exposed.\n\nBut I don’t know that it would go away completely, because I still see it myself. I know, rationally, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that when I’m alone or when I’m thinking clearly. But then when I’m at church, or playing bridge, it just starts up again — this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo if I had real certainty that nobody was evaluating me badly because of it, yes, I’d be less anxious. I think that would take away a lot of the fear. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out more than it should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it would decrease… probably quite a bit, honestly, if I really believed that they saw it and didn’t think any less of me. That’s the part that gets to me most, I think — not just that the scar is there, but that people might notice it and then have some thought about me because of it.

I do know, at least in my more reasonable moments, that the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can sort of tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge, it’s like I can’t keep that perspective. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if they’re just looking at my hand because I’m holding cards or reaching for something, I start thinking, “They saw it. They’re wondering about it.”

So if I knew they saw it but didn’t care, or didn’t think less of me, yes, I think I’d feel less anxious. I’d probably still be aware of it myself, because to me it still stands out, but it wouldn’t feel quite so humiliating or exposed.
