Like they’ll see the scar and their mind will go somewhere unpleasant, or they’ll think I look damaged somehow.\n\nI know, sitting here, that it probably isn’t as noticeable as it feels to me. I can tell myself that. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my wrist is out where people can see it, I get this strong feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now, before they notice anything else. And then I start imagining what they’re thinking, even if they haven’t said a word.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the scar on your wrist that you think makes it particularly unattractive or noticeable to others?\n\nPatient: I suppose it’s the way it stands out to me against the rest of my skin. I keep thinking it draws the eye, even if no one else says anything. It’s not that I can point to one single thing and say, “That’s it,” exactly… it’s more that when I see my wrist, my attention goes straight there, and then I imagine everyone else’s does too.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. It makes me feel very self-conscious.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you because of the scar itself or because they might be wondering how you got it?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the scar itself, really… though I suppose those two things get mixed together in my mind. I don’t always imagine someone thinking, “How did she get that?” so much as I imagine their eyes going straight to it, and then I feel exposed, like it’s the first thing about me that registers.\n\nI know, rationally, it’s probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can say that sitting here. But when I’m at church, or when I’m playing bridge and my hand is on the table, I get this feeling that everyone notices it right away. Like they see my wrist before they see me. And then whether they’re wondering how I got it or just thinking it looks unattractive… I don’t know, I start filling in the blanks myself. But the main thing is that I feel like they’re noticing it and judging me because it’s there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging your overall appearance because of the scar on your wrist?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think in a way I do. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. And once I feel that, I start thinking they must see me as less put-together, or less attractive, or just somehow marked by it. Like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I keep feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo I suppose the honest answer is that yes, I’d still be bothered by how it looks to me, but a lot of the distress comes from thinking other people must be seeing it the same way I do.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel self-conscious about your wrist scar if you were alone, without anyone else around to see it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would still feel self-conscious about it even if I were alone, though it’s different when no one else is there. When I’m by myself, I still see it. I’ll notice my wrist when I’m washing my hands or just sitting with my arm out, and it kind of pulls my attention to it. I know, in my head, that the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me, but it doesn’t always feel that way when I’m looking at it.\n\nBut it gets much stronger when I’m around other people. At church or when I’m playing bridge, that’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. I can look at my wrist and think it looks more obvious than it probably is, even when I’m trying to be reasonable about it.\n\nBut the worst part is when I’m with other people, especially at church or when I’m playing bridge. That’s when I start feeling like everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now. Even if no one says a word, I find myself assuming they’ve seen it and are thinking something about it.\n\nSo if I were in a situation where I somehow knew people weren’t judging me, I think I’d feel less tense and less exposed. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would still feel anxious about your wrist scar if you were in a private setting where no one could see it, but you were about to meet someone you admire or respect?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think I would. Maybe not in the exact same way as if they were already looking at me, but if I knew I was about to meet someone I really admire or respect, I’d start thinking about it ahead of time. Like, even sitting privately, I’d probably glance down at my wrist or imagine what it’s going to look like when I shake hands or sit across from them.\n\nI know the scar is probably not as bad as it looks to me — I can say that when I’m calm — but it still feels very noticeable to me. I know, rationally, the scar on my wrist is probably not as bad as it looks to me. I can tell myself that when I’m alone or when I’m thinking clearly. But then when I’m at church, or playing bridge, it just starts up again — this feeling that everyone’s eyes go right to it, like it’s the first thing they notice about me now.\n\nSo if I had real certainty that nobody was evaluating me badly because of it, yes, I’d be less anxious. I think that would take away a lot of the fear. But I’d probably still be aware of it, because to me it still stands out more than it should.\n\nDoctor: Would your concern about the wrist scar decrease if you knew that others saw it but did not think any less of you because of it?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would decrease… probably quite a bit, honestly, if I really believed that they saw it and didn’t think any less of me.
